[trialAccount t17800628-99]
403. ABRAHAM DAVIS was indicted for that he, together with thirty other persons and more, did, unlawfully, riotously, and tumultuously, assemble, on the 7th of June, to the disturbance of the public peace, and did begin to demolish and pull down the dwelling house of George Beckett, against the form of the statute, &c.
GEORGE BECKETT sworn.
I am a cabinet-maker, in Crown-court, Grub-street. My house was attacked, between four and five o'clock in the afternoon of Wednesday the 7th of June. I was in my garret. I heard a tumultuous noise; I ran down stairs; the first person I saw coming in at the door with that multitude was the prisoner at the bar; he had a bludgeon or stick in his hand, with which I saw him break the windows; he was the next person to me. I went out of the house and left him breaking the windows; and I saw no more of him. The mob demolished the furniture; they broke the closets and shelves down.
Did they break down the wainscoting? - The house has very little wainscoting in it; what there was they demolished.
Did you ever see the prisoner before? - No. I saw the prisoner again on Wednesday the 26th; I was coming home from Old-street; he was in White-Cross-street with a pea-cart; I immediately knew him again; he is so remarkable I could not mistake him; I am sure he is the man.
Did you mention that to any body before? - I mentioned it in my own family that one person struck me more than the rest.
PRISONER's DEFENCE.
The prosecutor swears I broke the windows I have but two fingers; I have persons to prove that I was at the Fleet prison from two till six o'clock.
For the Prisoner.
WILLIAM WILLIAMS sworn.
I keep an eating house in Grub-street; my father employed the prisoner to bring his goods from the Fleet; my father is in the Fleet; he came home and desired me to get some body to help to move his goods, as the Fleet was threatened to be set on fire. I went up to White Cross-street and got a cart. The prisoner was asleep in the stable; I waked him; that was a little before two o'clock.
What stables were they; - I do not know the name of the stables; there is a box maker at the corner of the yard. I drove the cart and he rode within side to Fleet-lane; it is a little potatoe cart.
Was the cart his? - Whether it was his or his master's I do not know; he mounted the cart while I went into the Fleet and helped to bring the goods down; when we had loaded the cart and came from it, it was past six o'clock; I believe the mob were then breaking Mr. Langdale's windows, because as we came to the top of the market we saw two men on the leads.
How far is Grub-street from the Fleet? - About a quarter of a mile.
Was he with you all the time? - Yes; from before two till almost seven o'clock; then I unloaded the cart.
Was you acquainted with him before? - No; my father bespoke the cart, and I went a little before two for it.
Cross Examination.
What evening was this? - Wednesday evening; the same evening the Fleet was on fire.
PATRICK WILLIAMS sworn.
I was a prisoner at the Fleet; on the Wednesday when this happened I was afraid of losing my little property; I ran to my children who were able to help me. I met my son William. I said come down and help me, as the Fleet is threatened to be set on fire; that was rather before twelve o'clock. I told him I had spoke to a man who keeps a little cart. I went back to pack up my things as fast as I could; about an hour or two after my two sons William and Thomas came; they helped to lead the things into the cart; I knew nothing of the prisoner before, only he was employed to carry my goods; he is a carman; he was employed from two o'clock. I did not get out of the Fleet till near six o'clock; I walked along by the cart all the way; the cart, I believe, belongs to one Armston; I know nothing of the prisoner; I spoke to Armston for the cart.
ANN ROWLAND sworn.
I saw the prisoner bring the goods from Mr. Williams's house on Wednesday the 7th of this month between six and seven in the evening.
Counsel for the Crown. There is so much doubt in this case that I desire the prosecution may proceed no farther.
NOT GUILTY.
Tried by the Second Middlesex Jury before Mr. Justice NARES.
[trialAccount t17800628-100]
404. DAVID EVANS was indicted for that he, together with forty other persons and more, did, unlawfully, riotously, and tumultuously, assemble, on the 6th of June, to the disturbance of the public peace, and did begin to demolish and pull down the dwelling house of the Right Honourable William Earl of Mansfield , against the form of the statute, &c.
THOMAS PARKER, Esq. sworn.
You are in the commission of the peace for the county of Middlesex? - I am. I went to Bloomsbury-square about three o'clock in the morning; the mob was very violent. I read the riot act at about half after three; I saw the prisoner with people, some of whom had bottles and bowls of liquor, which I imagined they got from Lord Mansfield's house, endeavouring to make the soldiers drunk; the mob was very violent; in order to keep them off from the soldiers it was thought necessary to order the soldiers to charge their bayonets; the men were drawn up from the corner of the iron rails which surrounded the grass, to the Duke of Bedford's wall; the prisoner was then with other people near the watch-house; the mob were very troublesome, and endeavoured to force the lines, but they were repulsed by the soldiers. I then went up to the prisoner and spoke to him, knowing the livery; I saw he was in liquor; I desired him to go home, and told him I was very certain Sir Thomas Sewell would be very angry at a servant of his for appearing in such a riot. He attempted to break through the rank a second time after that, whether he succeeded or not I cannot say. It was not his way home to break through the ranks, because his way home was either by the Archbishop of York's house, or he might have gone down a little street which comes from Southampton-row, and King-street.
Could he have gone by the archbishop's house? - Yes, that was left open. After I had done that I went to the Secretary of State's office, and saw Mr. Frazier; I told Mr. Frazier I was very sorry that there were so many servants concerned in that riot, amongst which I mentioned that I had seen a servant of Sir Thomas Sewell's.
What was the part which the prisoner seemed to take? - He seemed to be animating the mob.
During the time you saw the prisoner there, what were the rest of the mob doing? - Behaving very violent, ballooing, crying No Popery! throwing stones and glass bottles. One of the privates of the guards was wounded exceedingly, and Mr. Lowton was wounded with a glass bottle; the mob were then pulling down Lord Mansfield's house and burning the furniture.
Cross Examination.
The prisoner was in liquor? - Yes.
He had no arms nor any weapon with him? - I did not see him have any.
Captain BARNET sworn.
I was detached to Bloomsbury-square about ten o'clock; I remained there till five in the morning.
Do you remember at any time in the course of that night or morning seeing the prisoner, David Evans? - I do; I first saw him at half past one.
Did you observe him doing or saying any thing? - Not doing any thing, but talking much with other servants. There was a servant with him who was extremely insolent to me, and was abusing the guard.
How long do you think you saw the prisoner there? - I saw him there from half past one till five.
Had you occasion to observe his conduct at any other time? - Not particularly till the morning, when he attempted to pass the guard, and was told by them that he could not pass that way, he remained some time in front of the guard, then he forced his way through. Mr. Parker told him He knew his master and desired him to go about his business. Colonel Woodford, who was commanding officer at that time, and came with a detachment to assist me, said if he did not go on immediately about his business, he would take him into custody. I did not see him afterwards.
Cross Examination.
It was another person who was so abusive? - Yes.
He was outside the guard the whole time? - He was continually passing and re-passing my guard during the whole time, and he once came up and spoke to the serjeant of the guard; I told him to go away from the guard.
- ESCOURT sworn.
I was at Lord Mansfield's at about two o'clock; and I was there at three o'clock, and from then till between five and six in the morning. I was there when Mr. Parker came with a party of guards. Mr. Parker appointed me a constable; I remained there till between five and six in the morning, when the whole business was over. After the soldiers had fired between five and six o'clock, I think it was past five, I was called to by some of the people as a peace officer; they said, Sir, here is a servant making a great riot, I wish you would come and speak to him. The prisoner was then near the rails which surround the square on that side of the line of soldiers which came from the Duke of Bedford's wall to the rails; I went and told him I knew him very well, and I knew his master Sir Thomas Sewell. I advised him to go home, or I must take him into custody. He recollected me; he made no answer nor did I stay there any longer; I turned about and never saw him after.
At what time was that? - After the soldiers had fired; it was about five o'clock, I believe.
Court. Had you observed him before the people told you there was a servant very troublesome? - No, I had not seen him.
Cross Examination.
Then he went home? - I do not know; I saw no more of him.
Prisoner. I leave my defence to my counsel.
For the Prisoner.
Mr. CRAGGS sworn.
I am an officer belonging to the Master of the Rolls. The prisoner has lived servant with Sir Thomas Sewell ever since December. He came from Mr. Clare; I enquired his character of Mr. Clare; he gave him such a character as induced the Master of the Rolls to take him. He lived with Sir Thomas till he was taken up. I never heard any other than his behaving very decently and properly from every thing I have heard from Lady Sewell and the family; he is a decent, orderly servant.
Do you know that he went to see a lady from the house that night? - I do not live in the family; I have heard so.
Mr. CLARE sworn.
The prisoner lived with me twelve months; he was very diligent in his business; he kept very much at home. He was a sober orderly man, and a good servant; I had a very good character of him from his last place; they parted with him because they were going to live in the country. I did not give him warning, he gave me warning and left me to go to a better place.
Mr. Parker. Lady Sewell had such an opinion of him, as Captain Sibthorpe informed me, that she put great confidence in him; Captain Siothorpe gave that account, he is now in the country.
Mr. MAY sworn.
I have known him four or five years; he is a sober, honest, peaceable man; he lived servant with a relation of mine two years and an half, who always spoke exceeding handsome of him.
NOT GUILTY.
Tried by the Second Middlesex Jury before Mr. Justice NARES.
[trialAccount t17800628-101]
405. GEORGE SIMPSON was indicted for that he did unlawfully, maliciously, wilfully, and feloniously set fire to the dwelling-house of Victor Amada de la Tour, commonly called Marquis de Cordon .
2d Count. For setting fire to an out-house.
3d Count. For setting fire to the house generally, June 2d.
JOHN ROGERS sworn.
I am a brick-maker, and live in Coffin-court. I came by the Sardinian ambassador's house between eleven and twelve o'clock. The chapel is in Duke-street, the house is in Lincoln's-Inn-fields. I heard a noise in Duke-street; coming along I saw a fire in the street. I came and stood at the east end of the chapel to look, as other spectators did; I saw several people and the prisoner bringing out books, benches, and the lining of the chapel, and other things, and throwing them upon the fire.
Did you know the prisoner before? - I never saw him to my knowledge before that night.
Are you sure it is he? - Yes. I stood by some stables facing the chapel door; the prisoner came up to me, laid hold of me by the collar, and said, D - n your eyes, or blast your eyes, where are your colours!
What did he mean by your colours? - My not having a cockade in my hat I suppose. I told him I had seen Lord Mountmorres in trouble in Parliament-street, that I offered him my cockade, and his lordship desired me to give it to his coachman, as somebody else had given him one, which I accordingly did; I threw the prisoner over my leg and threw him down; I begged his pardon; then he left me and went to the fire; he took up five pieces of board, about the length of my arm, which were on fire, and set them against the right-hand door-post going into the eastern door, immediately after that the door-post was on fire. It was then reported that the military were coming.
Court. You do not know for certain that the fire was occasioned by the boards he set there? - It was not on fire before he set the boards there, but I saw the post burning afterwards. When it was reported that the military were coming, the people who were spectators absconded, and I absconded myself.
How long do you think you was there? - I had not my watch. I am sure I was not there a quarter of an hour.
How soon after was the prisoner taken? - I do not know; he was in custody soon after before the Lord-mayor for a misdemeanour, and was discharged.
Are you able to say from your observation of his person that he is the man? - Yes, he had a cut in his coat; he has not the same coat on now. I wrote a letter to Lord Hilisborough when things were settled and they began to take up people, describing the prisoner. I took him to be a shoemaker or blacksmith, by the long apron he had on. I sent the letter about the 11th or 12th day of the month.
Was you the first who gave information of him? - I cannot say, I told the constable if he saw such a person to take him.
Was it on your information he was taken up? - I believe it was; I got a party of soldiers, and he was taken to the London Hospital.
Did you know of any reward at that time? - No, I did not know of it till I heard it mentioned by Mr. Chetham here. I wrote to Lord Hillsborough that I wanted no see or reward.
Do you claim the reward? - No, I never shall; I do not want the reward.
Cross Examination.
What business are you? - A brick maker.
Do you follow that business? - Yes, on my stock; I have not made any this year; I made five thousand last year.
You was going accidentally by, and stood at the stable opposite? - Yes.
There was I suppose a great quantity of people? - No great quantity of people, there were several there.
You never saw the prisoner before? - I never say him before excepting twice. When he was taken up for setting fire to this place, I happened to be mentioning it to this constable.
Did you see a great number of boys and lads there? - Yes, and some men.
How came you to observe this man in particular? - I should not have taken notice of him if he had not come up and taken me by the collar. I took notice when I threw him over my legs that he had bandy legs.
He was taken up for some offence? - By Mr. Langdale's clerk I believe, but I did not know he was taken up.
How long was it before you charged him with this after you had found out the man? - I had information from the master he served his time with, where he was. When I found where he was I went to Mr. Alderman Wilkes; I got a party of soldiers and took him.
Court. How came you to find him out? - I was in a publick-house describing him, particularly to a constable, his size, his clothes and his legs, if he met with such a man to secure him, and send to me, and I would appear against him, for his impudence in collaring me. There were two persons sitting in a box, one said, that is our George. I did not take any notice only hearing him say it is our George, till the Monday following, then I happened to be in the house again about the hour of four o'clock, drinking a pint of porter; his master who was in the house on the Thursday before, happened to come in; he said he never heard a man describe another so near in his life as I described his servant. I asked him if he knew the man? He said yes, he served part of his time with him. I asked him where he was? He said there was no occasion to enquire, for he was far enough from here now, for he had been in prison and been discharged, and was far enough off by this time. With that I asked him if he had worked for him lately? He said no, the last place he worked at was for a widow in Shoe-lane, who was now married again. I enquired there for the prisoner, describing his person; she said his name was George Simpson, but he had been in prison, and was discharged, and she and her husband were not willing to discover where he was to be found. The next morning as her husband and I were going together to Mr. Alderman Wilkes, we happened to meet Mr. Wilkes in the street; I told Mr. Wilkes the whole affair; he granted me a warrant, and I got a guard of soldiers and took him up.
Jury. Was it not dark when you saw the prisoner first? - It could not be dark when there was a fire for the length of this sessions house.
He had a long apron on? - Yes.
How do you know he had crooked legs? - By throwing him down over my leg.
JOHN GOODRIDGE sworn.
I am a carpenter, in Shoe-lane; the prisoner lodged with me three months; on Friday night the 2d of June he left my house about nine in the evening and was not at home again till the next morning; I saw him the next morning between eight and nine o'clock. I asked him, George, where have you been all night? He said, Master I have been to the fire. I said George, you had better been at your work, or sitting for your work, and not lie out of my house. I asked what fire? He said, at the Danish ambassador's; he said, D - n my eyes, master, we have burnt it down, we have had fine fun! and then he said he would go home to bed; he said, if you live two or three days longer you will see half London on fire. He went home and went to bed; he lay till two or three o'clock in the afternoon; he got up again, and did a little work till about seven; he said he was so ill he could not do any more that night, but if I chose he would do a little in the morning to make his work up, which he did; he had some roll and tea for breakfast and went to bed on Sunday all day; on the Sunday night he came out about eight or nine o'clock; he said he would go out and get him something to drink and go to bed, which he did.
Cross Examination.
What are you? - A carpenter.
What is he? - A shoe-maker.
He worked for you? - Yes; I carried on both businesses.
This conversation happened on the 3d? - Yes.
Did not this strike you as a very wicked thing? - It did very much.
Did not you go to an alderman directly? - No, because it was only his words; I did not then, I did afterwards.
Then you went and gave him a good character? - No, we were afraid he had been concerned in oivers robberies.
When he was taken up why did not you tell the alderman this? - I thou ght it did not signify as it was only his words.
Can you read? - Yes.
You heard there was a reward before he was taken up? - No.
Did not you read it? - No.
The first time he was taken up he was discharged? - Yes; he was taken up for picking a gentleman's pocket; they asked if I was his master? I said yes.
Did he keep good hours? - No; and because I was his master he was discharged; that was all I was asked.
You live in Shoe-lane? - Yes.
How long have you lived there? - A twelvemonth.
He lay at your house? - Only one night after this.
How came you not to get rid of him? - At this time I was afraid of apprehending him.
Are you and your man upon an equal footing? - No.
He came and told you he had done this, and meant to burn down half London? - Yes; he told me this publickly in the street, but a few yards from the door.
Who heard this besides yourself? - I do not know that there was any body nigh me.
Perhaps he mentioned it to others as well as you? - Not that I know.
Who did you mention it to? - Mr. Wilkes.
Did you not tell it before? - No; I did not know any consequence could arise from his words.
You do not know that what a man says is evidence against himself? - No.
How came you to be a witness if you had not told it to any body? - I was sent for by means of Rogers coming to see for him.
Then you told him this? - No, I did not.
How came it into your mind to tell it at last? - When was before Mr. Wilkes he told me to tell all I knew of him, and then I told it.
You never read this proclamation which was pasted at the corner of every street? - I did not.
JOHN BATES sworn.
I am a carpenter.
Do you know the situation of the Sardinian ambassador's chapel and house? - Yes.
Is the Chapel joining to the house? - Yes, it is on the north side.
Is there a communication between the house and the chapel? - There is on the south side a covered passage, which leads from the south side of the house, from the one-pair-of-stairs; underneath that is a place for bottles, for the use of the butler; and there was a door into the chapel below stairs from the butler's closet, which is now closed up; there is also a communication on the north side which goes into the sacristary for the servants and other people to go into the chapel.
Does the chapel appear to be of equal date with the house? - The Chapel was built in the year 1763; it had been burnt down.
Is the chapel now burnt down entirely? - The north side of it is injured by the fire; the galleries are injured, and I believe six pillars were burnt and a part of the floor.
Cross Examination.
You are a carpenter? - Yes.
This chapel is a distinct building from the house? - There is a communication with the house.
As one house communicates with another? - There in an immediate communication with the vestry, which is a part of the house.
There is an eight foot passage between the house and the chapel on the south side? - Yes.
Then there is eight feet distance, I understand, between the house and the chapel? - There is; a projecting wing; I have made a sketch of it, if it will be any information to you (producing it).
Court. There are, I see, three communications; one up stairs from the ambassador's apartment to the chapel, one which has been stopped up, for the use of the servants, and one to the sacristary.
The chapel was built since the house?
Bates. In the year 1763, when the chapel was built, these communications, I under stand, were made, which were not before.
It might be separated from the house without injuring the house at all? - Yes.
Take away the chapel and make windows of the doors, and the house will be complete? - Yes, they were windows before; they are taken out and reversed and put on the south side.
As a builder do you suppose this chapel a dwelling house or a building adjoining to a house? - I look upon it as a building adjoining to a house.
LEONARD LOBAS sworn.
I am servant to the ambassador.
What is the ambassador's name? - Victor Amida, Marquis de Cordon de la Tour.
Was the Marquis in possession of this dwelling-house on the 2d of June? - Yes.
What is the ambassador's family name? - Victor Amida, Count de la Tour, Marquis de Cordon, his father is called Count de la Tour.
PRISONER's DEFENCE.
This Mr. Rogers says he never saw me before; he lives up the court next door to where I work; he used to bring jobs to me and stay while I did them.
To Rogers. The prisoner says he lives next door to you? - Yes.
Did you know he worked there? - No,
I never was in the house three times in my life; when I sent shoes to mend I always sent my children; I never saw him in the shop, to my knowledge, in my life.
For the Prisoner.
Mr. THOMAS THORPE sworn.
I keep the Globe-tavern in Fleet-street.
Do you know Rogers who has been examined as a witness? - About three months ago John Rogers applied to me to sign a paper in order to recover some money at the Assurance-office.
Give us an account of what is his general character? - The report was that he wilfully set his house on fire.
What is his general character? - He is a man of a very bad character.
Do you know any thing of the other man? - No.
Cross Examination.
Have you ever heard any thing against his character but what alludes to this transaction? - In our neighbourhood his character is very bad.
Has it arisen from this transaction - Yes.
Mr. JELLICO sworn.
I am an upholsterer in Fleet-street.
Have you known that man Rogers? - I never saw him in my life.
What is his character? - I know nothing of his character further than concerning a fire.
Do you know the other witness Goodridge? - I do not.
Mr. FENNING sworn.
I am first clerk in the Royal Assurance-office.
Do you know any thing of Rogers? - Only officially; we enquired his character in consequence of a fire.
Counsel for the crown. This certainly is not evidence.
Cross Examination.
Do you know any thing of him? - Only from this transaction; he is a very bad man and ought not to be believed.
Court. So far as your knowledge goes he is a man of bad character? - Yes. It is intended to prosecute him for perjury.
Why do not they do it? - The information is very recent.
- BRISCOE sworn.
I am a printer.
Do you know Goodridge? - I have seen him; he lives along with Mrs. Tiller; I do not know whether he is married to her.
Do you know any thing of his character? - I do not know any thing particular of his character; his character is very indifferent in the neighbourhood.
Do you know the prisoner? - He has been in my shop; I do not know much of him.
Cross Examination.
Is his bad character owing to his living with a woman of a different name? - I cannot say; I do not say it was from that.
WILLIAM FARDING sworn.
I have known Simpson upwards of five years; he lived with me; I had a very good character with him; I believe him to be a very honest lad; I have trusted him to receive money for me, and I never missed a farthing.
What are you? - I keep the Golden Horse, Aldersgate-street; he has left me three years.
Have you known him since? - Yes, he has always kept to work and never been beholden to any body I believe.
RICHARD KENDRICK sworn.
I am a plumber. I have known the prisoner four years; I never heard any thing against his character. I lodged in the same house. I never saw him in liquor, nor never heard any harm of him.
- READ sworn.
I am a shoe-maker, I have known Simpson these ten years; I never heard any thing amiss of him; he served me five years; he is a very honest lad; I never knew any thing to the contrary.
Cross Examination.
How long has he left you? - Five years.
Have you seen him frequently since? - Yes.
Have you had any reason to alter your opinion of him? - No.
RICHARD BENSON sworn.
I have known the prisoner from a child; he has a very honest general character; he was under my care eight years; I was guardian to him. He was apprentice to Mr. Read; I never heard any thing dishonest of him.
JOHN JOHNSON sworn.
I am a watch-maker. I have known him near fourteen years; he is strictly honest, and as to disposition, I believe, he is quite free from any thing of riot.
Cross Examination.
Do you live with him now? - No; I did eight or nine years ago; I have seen him frequently.
JOSEPH BRADDS sworn.
I am a peruke-maker. I have known him upwards of two years; I never heard any thing dishonest of him in my life.
JAMES FARMER sworn.
I have known him upwards of twenty years; I lived in the same house with him eight or nine years; I never heard any thing against his character till this time.
Cross Examination.
How long is it since you lived in the house with him? - I believe about twelve or thirteen years; I have seen him frequently since; I never heard any thing against him.
WILLIAM TUKE sworn.
I am a distiller. I have known him ever since he was born; I never heard an ill word of him in my life before this time.
NOT GUILTY.
Tried by the Second Middlesex Jury before Mr. Justice ASHHURST.
[trialAccount t17800628-102]
406. RICHARD EVANS was indicted for stealing sixteen pounds weight of brass, value 5 s. the property of John Reynolds, June the 26th.
THOMAS BIRD sworn.
On Monday the 26th of June I had been into Fore-street upon some business, between four and five o'clock; I went into the King's Head opposite Cripplegate Church; the prisoner and another man came in and called for a pot of beer; they sat down and drank. I observed the prisoner had something under his coat, which he frequently attempted to secrete; when he took out his money to pay for his beer I observed he had a large brass cock under his coat, what they call a stop cock. I suspected he had stolen the cock. I asked him what he was? He said he was a labourer. I asked him where he worked? Then he said he worked with Mr. Beardmore, did I know him? I said Beardmore and Reynolds in Barbican I knew very well. I went up to the landlord and told him my suspicion of the prisoner; then I went to Messrs. Reynolds and Beardmore and brought their clerk, Mr. Wilkins, with me to the publick-house. I showed him the prisoner and said that is the man. He said Richard, what is the reason you have not been at work? The prisoner said he had got too much liquor. Mr. Wilkins asked me if I was sure the prisoner had got the cock? I said he had. I went up to him and said, Friend, you have got a cock under your apron unless you have given it to the other person. The prisoner immediately took it from under his apron and laid it on the table. Mr. Wilkins asked him where he got that? He told him had it from the shop. He asked him what business he had with it? He said he had no particular business with it; he had taken it from the shop, and they might do what they would with him for they could not hang him.
Was he drunk or sober at that time? - He might have drank some liquor, but he was far from drunk at the time. Mr. Wilkins went and got a constable, and I stood with the prisoner while he was gone; some thing in his right hand coat pocket struck against the legs of a corporal in the militia. He said he had something else, and took it out of his pocket; it was a piece of metal; when that was taken out of his pocket I told the constable I thought it was his duty to search him; he was searched and we found another stop cock in his left hand pocket; then the constable said he thought it was necessary to send for his master, which we did. Mr. Reynolds came and talked to him about it; the prisoner again said, he had taken them; he might do what he would, he could not hang him.
- EDWARDS sworn.
I am a constable; the prisoner was stopped. I found a large cock on the table; the other cock and this piece of metal I took from him. (They were produced in court and deposed to by the prosecutor).
Reynolds. A piece of metal was found upon him which was broke off another piece which fitted exactly.
PRISONER's DEFENCE.
I tossed up with one of the men on Monday and got a little intoxicated; I have no witnesses.
GUILTY. W.
Tried by the Second London Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17800628-103]
407. THOMAS DOWNS was indicted for that he together with forty other persons and more, did unlawfully, riotously, and tumultuously assemble, on the 7th of June, to the disturbance of the publick peace, and did begin to demolish and pull down the dwelling-house of John Lebarty, against the form of the statute, &c.
ELIZABETH FRAZER sworn.
How old are you? - Going of fifteen. I am servant to Mr. Lebarty, in St. Catherine's-lane. I was a-bed in the house when the mob came; there was only my mother and me and another fellow servant in the house. I went to bed about eleven o'clock and went to sleep; my mother waked me. I got up and did not know which way to get down stairs the mob came up so fast; my room is adjoining to my master's; I was getting up at the head of the bed to get my petticoat and tumbled into my master's dining room where I saw the prisoner; I was very much terrified; I knew the prisoner a long time before; I went down stairs as soon as I could; the prisoner is one of Mr. Clark's runners; he was in the house that night and had change for half a guinea.
What were the mob doing at that time? - They broke the stairs below; they made their way up stairs as soon as they could.
What was the other part of the mob doing to the house? - They broke all the window frames and the brick wall down, and then they threw out the furniture, some of it was carried to the fire on Tower Hill, and some people carried some home to their own habitations; I did not go to see the fire.
Cross Examination.
When did you first charge this man? - My mother first charged him, and Mr. Sherwood's runners took him.
Do you recollect when he was taken? - No.
You and your mother told this immediately, I suppose? - Yes.
He was taken up immediately, I suppose? - No, he was not; he was before the justice about a week after.
Instead of going before the justices to charge this man you ran away and left him there without making any charge against him? - No, I did not go away, nor my mother, till we gave charge against him; Moses Benjamin, the constable, was the man who was with the prisoner at my master's house, at nine o'clock that evening, when I changed him half a guinea; I went and fetched change for half-a-guinea.
When you got up, when the mob came in, you were very much frightened? - Yes.
Why did not you go the next day to the justice? - We went to Benjamin, the constable, he was afraid to take him up; my mother did not know there was to be any law then.
Did you go to the justices more than once? - No.
You did not charge him then, did you? - Yes; we said we knew him.
Then you was not there once when you run away? - No, I did not run away.
Do you know a constable of the name of Whitway? - No.
Do you know the other constable you told it to, I do not mean Benjamin? - I do not know a constable of that name; I told it to no constable but Benjamin.
Did not you go in company with Lebarty, your mother, Mr. Whitway, and the prisoner? - Yes; I went with a man, I did not know his name was Whitway.
At the time you went with him, Lebarty, your mother, and the prisoner, did not you at that time run away and would not make a charge against the prisoner? - No.
Did not your mother and all of you refuse to make a charge against the man? - Yes; my mother did, because she wanted to speak to her master; Mr. Sherwood's runners said my master was coming, and he was not come; the two runners of Mr. Sherwood well knew where my mother lived, because they had some apprehension my mother knew something against the prisoner; my mother went with Mr. Sherwood's runners the first time they came about it.
Court. Then you went away without giving a charge? - Mr. Sherwood's runners ordered us over the way till my master should come to us; we waited there from six till seven, and my master never came; my mother and I went both over the way, the justice said he did not want to hear what I had to say, but he heard my mother.
When your master did not come you went over the way to the justice's without him? - Yes; the next day I was examined at the Rotation-office in Whitechapel.
Where was this? - I cannot tell, I was a stranger; I did not know the place.
To what justice was it you went, if it was not to Whitechapel, and where did he live? - I do not know the justice we went to then, but the next day I was examined at Whitechapel Rotation-office.
Don't you know where it was? - No.
Counsel. I mean a week before this when you went with Whitway to the Rotation-office, and you and your mother went away and the justice discharged him? - I went with two of Mr. Sherwood's runners; I do not know where to.
You know Mr. Sherwood very well? - Yes.
Counsel for the crown. How soon after your master's house was demolished did you first see your master? - I did not see him at all for four or five days after his house was destroyed; I did not know where he lodged or where he was; my mother and I went the next day to find him out, but we could not.
Where did you first tell this story? - In Whitechapel, at the Rotation-office.
When was the prisoner taken up? - I do not know.
To whom did you first give any evidence against the prisoner? - First I went to Mr. Benjamin, then to the Rotation-office in Whitechapel, where Justice Sherwood examined me.
Was that the first time you was examined? - Yes.
MARY FRAZER sworn.
I am the mother of the last witness. I lay at Mr. Lebarty's the night the mob came. I came about ten and went to bed about eleven o'clock; I went to sleep; when the mob came I jumped out of bed and opened the fore window; no sooner had I opened the window but there was a boy at the window; we had put lights in the window. I said I would open the door if they would not break it; before I could get to the top of the stairs the mob were up stairs; we lay in the back room; the mob thrust us into Mr. Lebarty's bed chamber; some boys opened the door of the fore room up one pair of stairs and the prisoner took a gun from the boy, and swore he would blow my heart out unless I would tell him where my master's money and papers were! after that another boy brought a sword or cutlass out of the same closet; the prisoner took that and pulled it out of the sheath; he held it over my head and said, he would split me in two if I did not tell him where my master's money and papers were. I said, gentlemen, don't do any mischief and cause murder, if you will go down stairs with me I will show you; the prisoner said I must stay a little till they had done their business there and then they would go down with me; we staid till they had destroyed what was in that room and thrown the furniture out of the window; I saw the prisoner beat out the frame of the window and throw the bed out I had laid on. He said D - n you, you old Papist bitch! come down stairs and show us where the money is; he several times called me a Popish bitch! I went down stairs; he followed me; I showed him the bar where Mr. Lebarty kept his liquor and money; I saw the prisoner go into the bar, and I came out immediately; that was the first opportunity I had of getting out of the house, and I saw no more of the prisoner; the back part of Mr. Lebarty's house was broke; whether he went that way or not I do not know; I saw him in the street by himself on the Sunday following in the forenoon. I knew him directly, because the chief thing I did in the room was to observe him. On the Sunday afternoon my daughter and I were in the house of one Mrs. Roberts, in East Smithfield; the prisoner then passed the door; I immediately told Mrs. Roberts that was the man who used me ill at Lebarty's; she said he is one of Justice Clarke's runners, and his name is Thomas Downs.
What did your daughter say upon that? - I believe she was in the yard playing with Mrs. Roberts's boy. That was not far from where I lodged. I went home and set down his name. On Monday I went to seek for Mr. Lebarty, and left word that I had seen one of the people who had been concerned in pulling down Mr. Lebarty's house, and had got his name; I went to one Mr. Brumett, a milk-man, in St. Catherine's, where I thought I might hear of Lebarty, and to a Jew officer, at the top of St. Catherine's-lane; I believe his name is Benjamin. As I was returning home, I met the beadle of Aldgate parish, and told him of it; he said why did I not go and make an information at the justice's. I went to the justice's; there was a guard of soldiers; I attempted to go in; they would not let me; that was on the Monday forenoon after the house was destroyed. My daughter was with me, there was a guard of soldiers brought from the Victualling office to Justice Clarke's in East Smithfield, to take a parcel of prisoners. I did not find Mr. Lebarty, till the Wednesday following.
When did you give the first information against the prisoner? - On the Wednesday at the Rotation-office in Whitechapel, which is almost opposite the church, I went with Lebarty; the Jew constable and the prisoner were there; as soon as I saw him, I said that is the man I saw in Lebarty's house, who pulled down the window. Lebarty, I, and my daughter went to the Rotation-office together.
Was your daughter with you when you endeavoured to get admitted to Justice Clarke's office? - She was.
Do you know any thing more? - I was sitting at the Fountain in the Minories; two of Justice Sherwood's runners came in a great hurry, and said Justice Sherwood had sent them for me.
When was that? - Some time after, I believe it was a week after; they said Lebarty was there waiting for me; my daughter was with me then. When I went there Lebarty was not there; I said I would not stay any longer, and then I was made a prisoner of, and was detained from my washing, in the publick-house, by these runners; I was kept till ten at night; then the justice examined me, and I swore to the prisoner.
Was your daughter examined then? - She was.
How long did your examination last? - Not a great while.
How long did your daughter's last? - Not long; we were only sworn that he was the person. I am certain and sure that he is the person.
Cross Examination.
You did not know him before but by sight? - Only by sight, my daughter knew him for many years.
Why did you enquire of any body else if you daughter knew him? - My daughter was not in the room when he used me ill with the gun and sword. She said she saw one of Justice Clarke's runners there, but then I did not know his name.
You knew Justice Clarke and Justice Sherwood too? - No, I did not.
You have been before them both before now? - No, I have been before Mr. Clarke, not Mr. Sherwood.
Your daughter has known this man's name for these two years? - Yes, I believe she did.
Why did not you apply to her? - She was not present when I saw him and he presented the gun and sword to me.
Did not you go to Mr. Clarke's to complain? - Yes, but was not admitted into the office. I did not go before Monday.
From the Wednesday to the Sunday, you never went to enquire after him? - I was not able to hold up my head hardly, I was so ill used in Mr. Lebarty's house.
How came you to recollect so readily that your daughter was at play at the moment the prisoner went by when you was at Roberts's? - I am not sure she was at play, I was stagnated at the sight of him.
If your daughter knew him before why did not you call her? - I was too stagnated at seeing him. I was at the Rotation-office, I believe it was on the Wednesday, and on the Thursday se'nnight.
Was your daughter there? - Yes.
Do not you recollect your daughter and you going away, and you would not give evidence? - No; I was there but I did not give evidence nor my daughter, because Mr. Lebarty did not choose to have any thing to do with the matter.
She knew the reason I suppose as well as you? - It was not the reason because I would not give evidence, but because Mr. Lebarty would have nothing to do with it.
So you and your daughter went away without giving any evidence? - We saw the prisoner, and said he was the person.
When you saw the prisoner there you made no charge against him nor your daughter? - No.
He was discharged? - No, he was not in custody, he was doing his duty, he had prisoners in charge I believe.
How soon after this was it that you chose to give evidence against him, it was a week after was it not? - I cannot tell, the justice's runners came to fetch me, it was some time after.
That was the 22d I believe? - I do not know, I saw no more of him till I was sent for.
If this man was so well known to your daughter, and the name told you by Roberts, how came you to go and get a pen and ink to write it down? - That I might not forget it.
But Roberts said he was one of Mr. Clarke's runners, and your daughter said the same, and she knew the name? - She knew him, I do not know that she knew his name.
You knew Justice Clarke's office, and Justice Sherwood's office, and all their runners very well I dare say? - No, I do not; I have been at Justice Clarke's office.
Have you all your warrants at Justice Clarke's office? - I never had a warrant in my life.
When you was so industrious to tell this in St. Catherine's-lane, why did not you go to the justice's? - I did not know the nature of law, I could not get in at the justice's.
When the runners came for you you say you was in prison, what was that for? - They said they would keep me till they went and took Thomas Downs.
Was you locked up? - No, I was in the room, they would not let me go out.
And your daughter too? - Yes.
Was not that because you had run away before? - No, I never ran away before.
Do you know Whitway? - No, I do not know him by name.
Court. Where was your daughter from the Wednesday the house was destroyed to the Sunday? - In lodgings with me all the time.
I suppose you naturally had a good deal of talk respecting that terrible night? - At that time I was too ill to be talked to. I was greatly hurt in Mr. Lebarty's house; I did not talk about the rioters all that time that I know of. My nose began bleeding from that time to Sunday; then it stopped bleeding, and I went out to Mrs. Roberts's.
Was you speechless all that time that you spoke nothing about it? - No, but my nose kept bleeding; I had a blow on the back of my head; we might speak that there were such riots, but not to any purpose.
When did she tell you she saw one of justice Clarke's runners there? - On the Friday she told me she saw some runners, some butchers, and Jews, in Mr. Lebarty's house.
Though she told you on Friday she knew one of Justice Clarke's runners was there, you never once asked her if she knew his name? - No.
You have heard I suppose there is money to be got in this way? - I never heard there was money to be got this way, I know nothing of it.
If you convict this man you are entitled to fifty pounds? - I do not want the fifty pounds.
Who told you you were not to wish to have the fifty pound, the attorney? - No, I heard the runners were to have fifty pounds for taking them.
Did not you expect to have some of that fifty pounds? - No.
Did you hear no talk of it? - No.
Did not your friend Moses the constable tell you of it? - No, he is no friend of mine.
You say that the boy the man took the gun from brought it out of the closet and the sword too? - Yes.
Whose property were they? - Mr. Lebarty's, I believe.
Counsel. Was your daughter in the room when the prisoner did what you speak of? - No, she went down stairs.
You was not in this room till your daughter went down stairs? - Yes, when she went down stairs.
What did the man do with the gun and the sword? - Threw them out at the window.
JOHN LEBARTY sworn.
Did you see the prisoner at any time on Wednesday the 7th of June? - Yes, I saw him, I think he was at my house to the best of my remembrance, between eight and nine o'clock, he came to get change for a half guinea.
That was on the evening your house was demolished? - It was.
Did you go to the justice's with Mary and Elisabeth Frazer, to give information against the prisoner? - I did not give information against the prisoner, I was summoned by the justice to go there, and I went.
What time was that? - I do not recollect the day, I was so disturbed in my mind I cannot recollect.
Did you say any thing to Mary Frazer for her not to give information against the prisoner? - I spoke that I would let it alone, I would have nothing to do with it, as I was a stranger in the nation I thought I would let it alone.
Cross Examination.
You say you saw him between eight and nine o'clock, you are not exact to the time I suppose? - I do not know, he and two more came in for change for half a guinea.
You knew him before? - I certainly did.
Your servant knew him and knew his name? - I believe so.
Court. How came you to leave your house so early that afternoon? - A message was brought to me that the mob were going to burn my life, therefore I left the house between ten and eleven o'clock.
You did not think of arming yourself against them? - What could I do, they were all against me?
Had you any arms in the house? - I left a gun in the closet I believe.
Prisoner. I leave my defence to my counsel and my witnesses.
For the Prisoner.
WILLIAM WHITWAY sworn.
Do you know Mary and Elisabeth Frazer? - I have seen them before. On Thursday the 15th of June, in the morning, they came to the King's-Arms, East Smithfield, opposite Justice Clarke's office; they and Lebarty came together, and began to relate the matter about the prisoner to me and some others; I told her I had seen him that morning, and if I had known it I would have stopped him. I understood she was going up to the Rotation-office about some other business concerning the rioters.
Who said so? - I do not know, I believe all three, that they were going up concerning the examination of other rioters. I was going up there myself. I told her to go up and I would endeavour to find Downs, and if I found him I would bring him up.
What did they say to that? - They said it was very well; if I found him to bring him up. I went to his house; he happened not to be at home; as I was going I saw him on the opposite side of the way; I beckoned him; he came to me; I told him what the woman said; he declared he was innocent, and went with me voluntarily. When he came there the women were at the Rotation-office; there were some altercations between them, but the particulars I cannot remember; there were Justice Sherwood and Justice Clarke; I related the story to them; the magistrates ordered me to bring the prisoner up; I did so. When I brought him up, I thought the woman was following me; when I came up she was not there. The magistrates asked where the woman was who was to make the charge? I went down to look for her, and the woman and her daughter and Lebarty were all gone out of the house. The magistrate then dismissed him, and desired he would be ready to be called for whenever he was wanted. He told them he would. He then went away about his usual business, and I heard no more about it till the Wednesday following, which I think was the 21st, then the woman went down to Mr. Sherwood's and laid an information, and Mr. Sherwood's people came and took him. He was at large all that time up and down with the prisoners; he is a servant to Mr. Newport.
JOHN SADLER sworn.
I attend the publick-office in East Smithfield.
Do you know Downs? - Yes.
Do you remember the night Lebarty's house was burnt? - Yes, Wednesday the 7th of June. I was in company with the prisoner and Wilkinson that night at the Ship, till ten o'clock. Mr. M'Kenzie keeps it; I left the prisoner there.
Cross Examination.
You attend the publick-office? - Yes, as an headborough of the parish of Aldgate. I went to the Ship that evening between the hours of nine and ten; he staid with me from that time till near twelve.
That was the first time you saw him that evening? - No, I saw him before in East Smithfield, pretty near the Maypole at the King's Arms. I made an agreement to meet him at the Ship? - About nine, or a little after, and staid with him till near twelve.
Who were with you? - John Wilkinson, Sarah Aldin, Sarah Hart, and Mr. M'Kenzie.
Who did the prisoner go away with? - I cannot tell, I left him there; Wilkinson went with me.
Court. How far is the Ship in East Smithfield from Lebarty's house? - Three or four hundred yards. As I was going home I met one of Lebarty's lodgers; he called to us and said the mob were pulling Libarty's house down. I said if we can be of any service I will get our men to try and disperse the mob; he said it was of no use; they were then pulling it down.
JOHN WILKINSON sworn.
I was at the Ship the night Lebarty's house was pulled down; I was there between nine and ten in company with Sadler, Downs, Mr. M'Kenzie, Armstrong and his wife, Sarah Aldin and Sarah Hart. I left the house about a quarter before twelve o'clock; going home we met one of Lebarty's lodgers; he said they were pulling Mr. Lebarty's house down.
Cross Examination.
What are you? - A runner.
Did the prisoner go with you? - No, we found him there.
What liquor did you drink there? - Nothing but porter. We drank I suppose four or five pots; I paid for one. We left him there.
MARY M'KENZIE sworn.
I keep the Ship, a publick-house.
Do you know Sadler, Wilkinson, and Downs? - Yes, they were at my house on Wednesday night the 7th of June. Downs came in about nine and staid till about half after twelve o'clock; just about half after twelve he desired to have another pint of beer, and paid me for it; I saw him to the door and saw him turn round the corner to go home; he went home with Mr. Hart.
Cross Examination.
When did Sadler and Wilkinson come into your house? - Between nine and ten o'clock.
What time was Lebarty's house pulled down? - I cannot tell. About half after twelve they said there was a great fire on Tower Hill; that the mob were pulling down a house in St. Catherine's-lane.
People came in after nine o'clock? - Yes, some.
A great many? - No; our house is not so overflowed with business.
You could not see Lebarty's house from your house? - No; only the blaze of the fire on Tower Hill.
THOMAS ARMSTRONG sworn.
My wife and I were at the Ship the night Mr. Lebarty's house was destroyed; we went there between seven and eight o'clock; the prisoner Downs came in I believe rather before nine and staid till near twelve; we left him there; the watch was going twelve as we came out.
Cross Examination.
What are you? - A lighterman, and live in Artichoke-lane at the bottom of Virginia street.
Did you see Sadler there? - Yes.
Was Wilkinson there? - Yes.
Did they all come in together? - No; Downs came in first, I believe; Sadler and Wilkinson came in together.
What liquor did the prisoners, Sadler and Wilkinson, drink? - Beer, I believe; I did not see any thing else.
Did you see the fire at Lebarty's? - I did not see it; I d id not hear of it till the next morning, if I had gone out perhaps I might have seen it, but I did not take any notice about it.
Were there a good many people in the Ship? - I cannot say how many.
Twenty or thirty? - I cannot say.
Did no-body say any thing to you about Lebarty's house being destroyed that night in the Ship? - No, no-body said any thing to me.
SARAH HART sworn.
I was at Mr. M'Kenzie's, the Ship, the night Mr. Lebarty's house was destroyed; the prisoner and I went home together at about half after twelve o'clock.
What became of him then? - He went to bed and did not get up till between seven and eight next morning.
Cross Examination.
You live with the prisoner? - Yes; I have lived with him nine years.
Are you married to him? - No.
- CLARKE sworn.
I am servant to Mrs. Hart.
Do you recollect what time your master and mistress came home the night Lebarty's house was destroyed? - Yes; they came home, as near as I can guess, at half after twelve o'clock, and went to bed; I locked the door; the prisoner never went out again till seven or eight o'clock in the morning, for I had the key in my pocket.
Cross Examination.
How came you to be so exact to the time? - The watch hung up, and I saw what time it was when he went to-bed.
HANNAH SMYTH sworn.
I work at my needle, and lodge at Mrs. Hart's.
Do you recollect at what o'clock Sarah Hart and Downs came home on the night Lebarty's house was destroyed? - Between twelve and one, I was up at the time.
Who was the first who got up in the morning? - I was; Mr. Downs got up about eight or rather sooner.
Cross Examination.
Do you always take notice what time Downs goes to bed and gets up? - Being always in the house I do.
What time did Mrs. Hart go to bed last night? - I believe about the same hour.
What time did she get up this morning? - About eight o'clock.
What are you? - I work at my needle, or any thing else I can get to do among the neighbours.
- NEWPORT sworn.
I am keeper of New-Prison; the prisoner has been a servant of mine three or four months; he has behaved with the strictest honesty, according to the best of my knowledge; he was very attentive to my business; he was at Justice Clarke's office when he came to me.
How long have you been keeper of New Prison? - Six or eight months.
NOT GUILTY.
Tried by the Second Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.

WILLIAM DOWNS was indicted, for that he, together with 40 other persons and more, did unlawfully, riotously and tumultuously assemble, on the 8th of June, to the disturbance of the publick peace, and did begin to demolish and pull down the dwelling house of Cornelius Murphy, against the form of the statute, &c.
(The counsel for the crown did not call any witnesses in support of this indictment.)
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t17800628-105]
409. WILLIAM GOODCHILD was indicted for stealing 4 lb. of black pepper, value 6 s. the property of William Smith, William Smith Campbell, Joseph Nash, and Joseph Travers, June the 29th.
Mr. JOSEPH TRAVERS sworn. I am a grocer, and am in partnership with Messrs. Smiths and Nash, grocers; the prisoner was a servant of our's. On Thursday the 29th of June, as the prisoner was going home to dinner, the warehouse man detained him, saying, he had something in his pocket. I took him into the cellar and asked him what he had in his pocket? He replied nothing at all. I lifted up the flap of his pocket and saw the pepper and asked him what that was? He artfully replied I have been working this morning without my coat; and one of my fellow servants, having a malice against me, has done this. I replied, that would not do; I was happy I had discovered the thief for the sake of the reputation of my other servants, and that he must come along with me. He instantly threw off his coat and said, The pepper was not his property, and he would not have the property of another about him. I laid hold of the coat with one hand and the man's arm with the other, left he should get away; and turning round to the cellar door I observed my partner, Mr. Campbell, coming towards me. I said Mr. Campbell this is the man who robbed us; while I was conversing with him the prisoner slipped out of my hand, and escaped into the lane by another door of the cellar, and left his coat behind him; we all pursued him into Thames-street, where we missed him; then we divided, and when I returned he was brought into the compting-house. Mr. Campbell called upon him to confess, but prior to his making confession, he was told that the way to expect favour was to discover his associates. My lord will it be proper to mention his confession after that.
Court. It will not; you do very right to mention it.
What is the value of the pepper? - Six shillings.
WILLIAM DUETT sworn.
I am warehouse-man to Messrs. Smith, Nash, and company. On Thursday was se'nnight I ordered the prisoner to go to dinner; he was at work without his coat; he went backwards to the place where his coat lay, and staid about ten minutes; when he came forwards I met him and saw his pockets were very full, and could discern it was pepper. I called Mr. Travers and he took him into the cellar to search him.
Do you know where his coat had been lying? - Yes; upon some lump sugar in the back part of the warehouse. I had an information of him and concealed myself in the desk. I had sent all the other men to dinner before him, in order to see whether he would do it or not; there was no-body there but our cooper's little boy in the front shop; when the men were gone to dinner I went and put my hand in his pockets and they were empty.
Had the boy been backwards after that time? - I believe not.
Prisoner. If you can say upon you oath there was no man went backwards into the warehouse after you felt in my pockets and had sent the men to dinner? - I am upon my oath, there was not one.
AARON COATES sworn.
I am a constable; hearing the cry
"Stop thief," I helped the last witness to secure the prisoner and bring him back.
PRISONER's DEFENCE.
My lord, and gentlemen of the jury, I was recommended to the prosecutor by one of the first merchants in London. I served him a considerable time as warehouse-man, and quitted his servitude with a character unblemished. I served the prosecutor with the same integrity, but with fellow servants who were full of malice against me, and to complete their diabolical design have done this; finding my coat heavier than it used to be I put my hand into my pocket and found it was black pepper. I thought it was done out of a joke, but I found it was meant to destroy me. I went to put it out into the tub but I thought I would go to Duett to show it him in order to find the person out who had put it into my pocket; he told me immediately that Mr. Travers wanted to speak to me in the cellar about some fruit. I went down into the cellar; he asked me what I had in my pocket, and threatened to hang me. I thought it was to be done in the cellar, and therefore I made my escape but was taken and brought back; and then they talked again of hanging me; there was a soldier's bayonet lying; I thought they were going to put an end to my existence. I begged they would suffer me to be tried by the laws of my country. Mr. Travers then promised I should, and sent for a constable. I have a disconsolate wife near the time of her delivery. I leave myself to the mercy of the jury, trusting they will make a just sentiment.
Mr. Travers. I did not say I would hang him, nor to my knowledge I did not threaten him.
GUILTY. Imprisoned 6 months.
Tried by the Second London Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17800628-106]
410. SARAH COLLOGAN was indicted for stealing a printed cotton gown, value 20 s. the property of Elisabeth Murray, spinster, June 6th.
JAMES HYDE sworn.
I belong to the Rotation-office in Litchfield-street; I was at Lord Mansfield's house with the magistrates a little before four o'clock on the Wednesday morning on which the house was destroyed; at about half after four I had an information that some of Lord Mansfield's property was at the house of the prisoner. I went to the house, and told the prisoner I was come to look for some things which came from Lord Mansfield's. While I turned my back to look in the lower room she went to the stair-case; I followed her immediately up stairs.
Who gave you the information? - I took two people who used to lodge in the house with some of the property on them; they gave me an information that there were more things in the house. I saw the prisoner put this gown under the head of the bed; I laid hold of her and the gown together. She said that was all she had; she said it was given her by a man she did not know at Lord Mansfield's house. This is the gown (producing it.)
HENRIETTA CASSMAJOR sworn.
I am lady's woman to Miss Elizabeth Murray; I know it to be her gown, having made it myself; I saw it in the house at ten o'clock over night, it was locked up in a press.
PRISONER's DEFENCE.
I went that night to see the fire, as many more did; a man came down the stairs with an arm full of linen and this gown; he put the gown up to my face, and said if I would not have it he should throw it into the fire; he threw the rest of the things into the fire.
GUILTY. Imp. 12 months.
Tried by the First Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17800628-107]
411. ELIZABETH GRANT was indicted for stealing a copper pot, value 30 s. a copper-plate warmer, value 3 s. and a copper dripping-pan, value 10 s. the property of the Right Honourable William Earl of Mansfield , June 6th.
JOHN DIXON sworn.
I belong to the Rotation-office in Litchfield-street; I went to the house of one Mr. Gocart, in Hog-lane, three or four days after the fire at Lord Mansfield's, there I found these copper things (producing the several articles mentioned in the indictment). Gocart said he knew nothing of them. The prisoner said she bought them when he was out of the way. I believe she is a servant in the house; it is an old iron-shop; I found some on the compter and some behind it, and some were in the back shop; I took the goods, and left the prisoner. Two or three days after that, when Lord Mansfield's servants came to town, and saw these things, they said they were Lord Mansfield's, I went and told the prisoner she must come to the office. She said she would come as soon as she had put on her cap; she came there soon after me.
JAMES HYDE sworn.
I went with Dixon to Gocart's; we found this copper, some in the fore and some in the back shop.
( George Wilkinson and Henry Richards, two of Lord Mansfield's servants, deposed, that the several articles produced were the property of the Earl of Mansfield.)
PRISONER's DEFENCE.
I am servant to Mr. Gocart. Four or five fellows came in with blue cockades, and brought this copper; I said I did not buy any thing; my master keeps an open shop. They said if I did not buy them they would say we were papists, and would have the house down. They demanded money; I gave them what money I had, and should have given them more, if I had had it in my pocket. When my master came home he asked me how they came there; I told him. He said, Let them lie there, for I suppose they come from some of the demolished houses. The next day he went out to market, and the officers saw the things and took them. There is a person here, who lodges in the house, that saw the men in the shop.
For the Prisoner.
SARAH PHILLIPS sworn.
I lodge in the house where the prisoner lives. On the 7th of June, between eleven and twelve o'clock, while I was in my chamber, I heard a noise, I came down stairs, and saw five or six men in the house with blue cockades; they insisted upon the prisoner buying these things; she refused to buy them. They told her, if she did not buy them they would set fire to the house, because they were papists.
Is the man of the house a catholick? - Yes, he is a Flemish man.
Did she buy the things? - I went up stairs to look after my children; when I came down stairs again I saw the woman give them money, but I do not know how much.
Prisoner. I have somebody here to prove my master is ill of the gout.
JOHN GRAY sworn.
I am an apothecary in Bartholomew-Close, I attend the prisoner's master, he was ill of a fever; the fever is now gone off, and he has a white flux.
The prisoner called John Slaide and - Majorby, who gave her a good character.
NOT GUILTY.
Tried by the First Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17800628-108]
412. ELIZABETH TIMMINGS was indicted for stealing five china dishes, value 20 s. five china plates, value 5 s. a linen sheet, value 8 s. a linen napkin, value 10 d. a pair of leather gloves, value 1 s. and a muslin cap, value 2 s. the property of the Right Honourable William Earl of Mansfield , June 6th.
JOHN DIXON sworn.
I found the things mentioned in the indictment (producing them) in Timmings's house; we had an information that a great many things were carried to that house; we found her packing some of them up; we took her into custody; when she was at the office she said she had had them a great many years; she said the sheet was given her by an aunt of her's in Ireland; she said she bought some of the china at Bristol, and had had the things in general a great many years; that they were her own property; that she took the sheet to Ireland, and brought it back with her.
Cross Examination.
This confession was taken down in writing, was it not? - I believe not.
Did not she tell you that Ann Charlotte, Mary Gardener, and Esther Kelly, brought these things there? - No.
You took every thing you could lay your hands on, and her own things too? - Yes; I do not know that she had so much of her own.
Was there not an application made to the court to deliver her her things. You never heard that Charlotte, Gardener, and Kelly, had given her the things? If they had given her the things, why did not she say so? they were in the house at the time.
Have they not been charged with this offence? - No.
You never heard her charge these three people with bringing the things? No.
JAMES HYDE sworn.
I belong to the office in Litchfield-street.
Was you at Timmings's at any time? - Yes; I am not positive to the time; we searched the back room, and found a parcel of linen and some china. I took her into custody. I think she said they were her own things, and that she had them from some relation. About three or four days after she had been committed I was at the jail; she said she wanted to speak to me; we went into the wine-room in Tothill-fields; she said Ann Charlotte, or Charlotte Ann, I cannot say which, brought the things to her; that girl was in the house when I took the things. I advised her to send to the magistrate; I believe she afterwards sent to the magistrate.
Cross Examination.
Did not she tell you that Ann Charlotte, Mary Gardiner, and Esther Kelly, brought the things? - No; but I was told something of that was mentioned at the office. Ann Charlotte, I believe, was taken up, but it was for something else. She was put to the bar for somebody to look at her, and she went away again.
GEORGE WILKINSON sworn.
I am servant to Lord Mansfield; I believe all these things are my lord's property.
Was you present when the prisoner was examined at the justice's? - Yes; she said that she had had some of them from Ireland, and others a great while, but I cannot say the time; they were in Lord Mansfield's house on Tuesday the 6th; they are plates that are made use of every day.
HENRY RICHARDS sworn.
I am cook to Lord Mansfield. I know the two sallad dishes, and two of the plates, to be my lord's property; they were in the house on the Tuesday night; the prisoner, upon her examination before the justice, said, she had had some many years in Ireland, and some from Bristol.
HENRIETTA CASSMAJOR sworn.
I am Miss Murray's maid. I know the linen to be the property of Lord Mansfield, by having often seen it there. I am certain of it.
Was you present when the prisoner was examined before the justice? - I was. I understood her in general to say, that they had been in her possession some time, I do not know how long, some were given her by a relation, and sent from Ireland. They were in Lord Mansfield's house on the 6th of June.
PRISONER'S DEFENCE.
When I was taken up I told them Ann Charlotte and Mary Gardiner brought the things to me, and desired they would go and take them, and I would pay them for bringing them the next morning to me, but they did not bring them. When I went to Tothill-fields there were two men; we sat on the bench; I was telling them how I was brought in by those people; they said if I brought them, and swore against them, I should be cast myself. I said what must I do; they said, say you had them from Ireland, or any where; so I did, and they laughed at me.
WILLIAM TILCOCK sworn.
I am a driver belonging to the Artillery. I lodged at the prisoner's house in June.
Do you remember the night Lord Mansfield's house was burnt? - Yes When I went to bed at ten o'clock she was at home.
Cross Examination.
How came you to recollect that night? - Because the next morning I went off early to camp.
Did you see any thing of Mrs. Tilcock after you went to bed? - Yes, between twelve and one she came into my room; I heard the lodgers come in.
Does she lie in the same room? - No, in the next room.
Does any body lie with you? - Yes, Perry.
Does any body else lie in the same room? - Yes, another woman; Perry lodges there.
SARAH PERRY sworn.
I lodge in this house. Tilcock lodges there. The prisoner was at home all that evening, all night, and next morning between twelve and one o'clock. Ann Charlotte, Poll Gardiner, and Esther Kelly came in and asked the woman where I lodged if t hey should leave the things; there was a sheet, some china, and some trifling things, I cannot recollect what, I was in bed there; she said they should not leave them then. They said where should they leave them but in their lodgings? that they came honestly by them, and would leave them there.
What sort of china was it? - Long dishes, two soup plates, and brown plates all full of colours; it was burnt-in china I believe.
Cross Examination.
What are you? - I have been out of place three months; I have lodged with Mr. Timmings ever since.
Who lodges in this house besides? - Several young men; sometimes strangers every night.
How came you to take such particular notice of that night? - Because I looked at the things particularly.
How came you not to recollect these things when those gentlemen asked you about them? you said there were a sheet and china, and some trifling things you could not recollect? - Some cups and such things; I did not recollect the china; I was always with Timmings every night.
Have you been there ever since you lodged with her? - Yes, I have never been out of the house since.
Court. Are you sure they brought all the things which were brought? - Yes.
Did they bring the silk gown there? - No, I must have seen it if they had. I was never out of the place.
Did you ever see the watch brought there? - No, I did not. Some things were taken away in the morning; these were kept.
MARY HARGRAVE sworn.
I lodge in this house too; I was there between twelve and one o'clock, on the 6th of June. Ann Charlotte, Gardiner, and Kelly knocked at the door; Mrs. Timmings let them in; they brought some things with them, and desired she would let them leave them there. She refused. They said they came honestly by them and where should they leave them but in their lodging. After some persuasion she let them bring them, and they left them below stairs, and went up to bed; she called to them afterwards to take them away. There was a cloak with fur on it, two sheets, one longer than the other a petticoat, and some other linen.
Did you see any plates there? - Not till next day. She was going to bed when they came in. She was not out that evening.
NOT GUILTY.
Tried by the First Middlesex Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
[trialAccount t17800628-109]
409. ELISABETH STEWART was indicted for stealing twenty nine yards and an half of thread lace for ruffles, value 10 s. a muslin handkerchief laced, value 20 s. a pair of treble lawn ruffles, value 10 s. twenty-four yards of lace, value 10 l. a child's cloak laced, value 10 s. seven yards of silk, value 30 s. a sattin gown and coat, value 10 s. a muslin apron, value 5 s. another apron, value 2 s. a sattin cloak, value 3 s. a man's camblet riding cloak, value 3 s. a linen gown, value 5 s. a black silk handkerchief, value 2 s. a necklace made with stones, set in silver, value 5 s. a cotton gown, value 5 s. a cotton work-bag, value 1 d. and four pounds ten shillings, in monies, numbered, the property of Margaret Holbird, widow, April, 9th.
MARGARET HOLBIRD sworn.
I live in Mitre-court, Leadenhall-street. The prisoner was my servant. On Sunday morning, the 9th of April, I went into the country, and left a Mrs. Hussey and the prisoner to take care of the house. I came home on the Wednesday, and found the maid was gone; Mrs. Hussey told me she went on the Monday. I did not miss any thing then. The next day, Thursday, about five or six o'clock in the afternoon, I went to the closet and could not at first open it; at last I got it open, and immediately missed my box of money. I then missed my handkerchief and laced ruffles, and the next morning I missed my bag with all my lace in it. I then went to enquire after the prisoner; I found she had been at Mrs. Cole's, a dyer in Ratcliff-highway; they informed me she had fetched some things out of pawn which were at a pawnbroker's opposite. I went to the pawnbroker's to know if the prisoner had fetched any thing out of pledge, and enquired in what money she had paid for them? They said all in half crowns; my money was all in half crowns, except a five shilling and three penny piece of George I. I heard she was gone on board a ship; I got a warrant to go on board to find her. I sent a man to enquire, and was informed the ship was gone. I then concluded it was all over, and thought no more of it. On the 24th of May a young man came and told me that the person who had roobed me was in the Compter. I went to the Compter and saw the prisoner. I asked her about the things; she owned the fact; she said she had the lace; that she had sold the handkerchief and cloak to Ann Fieldhouse for three shillings, and Mrs. Fieldhouse had pawned the remainder of the things.
Did you make use of any promise to induce her to confess this? - No; after she had confessed, I said, I want nothing but my things, if I get them I will do you all the service I can. I got a search warrant to take up Mrs. Fieldhouse. I went to Ann Fieldhouse's to look for the things, and a constable with me, as soon as I got the warrant. I believe I found my child's muslin cloak cut and part made into a cap, the remainder was there; it was a muslin cloak worked with lace all round it; I found my bag there.
Was the prisoner there at the time? - No, she remained in the Compter; I went to a pawnbroker's and there I found five yards of my silk, and three pieces of lace; they denied every thing else. I went in a coach with Mrs. Fieldhouse to her sister's; there I found a gown and coat. The prisoner had told me that the person where she lodged had sold all the things to Mrs. Fieldhouse's sister.
When was it the prisoner told you this? - The first time.
Before you had made her any promise? - I made no promise till the next day, I went again. I went to Fieldhouse's sister on the Friday night. She keeps a pastry-cook's shop on Snow-hill; her name is Jane Thompson. I found there a garnet colour sattin gown and coat, and a black cloak pulled all to pieces, and a necklace.
JANE THOMPSON sworn.
I have here some things I bought of Mrs. Clark, by the consent of the prisoner, who was present; she said they were her's; the prisoner lodged with her. She told me that they were her mother's and that she brought them from Ireland, that Mrs. Clark had fetched them out of pawn, and was to keep them till she could pay for them.
Are these the things you bought of Clark by the prisoner's desire? - Yes, no more nor less.
(They were produced in court and deposed to by the prosecutrix.)
JOHN CRAWFORD sworn.
I am servant to Mr. Windsor. I have about three yards and a half of silk and two remnants of lace; they were pledged at our house in the name of Ann Fieldhouse. I never saw the prisoner.
(They likewise were produced in court and deposed to by the prosecutrix.)
- HUSSEY sworn.
Mrs. Holbird went into the country, on the 9th of April, about nine o'clock; as I worked for her, she desired me to stay in the house with the prisoner till she came home; the prisoner went away, on Monday morning at eight o'clock; she told me she was going to a gentleman, expecting to get some money to carry her over to Ireland, she would come back before dinner, but she did not come back.
ABRAHAM SOLOMONS sworn.
I am a constable. On the 24th of May Mrs. Holbird brought me a warrant for Ann Fieldhouse. I have here the things which were found in Ann Fieldhouse's house; here is a bag, some pawnbroker's duplicates, and a white cloak; the duplicates were brought to the Alderman; they were first pawned in the name of Fieldhouse, and then altered to the name of the prisoner; when she came before the alderman she brought the lace with her. (That likewise was produced and deposed to by the prosecutrix.)
PRISONER's DEFENCE.
On the Monday I went to Mrs. Clarke's. I was there three days; then I went to Mrs. Fieldhouse's, and she took and pawned the things; I gave her the laced handkerchief to pawn for seven shillings, and she would not own it afterwards. I have no witnesses; I rely upon the mercy of the court.
GUILTY. Imprisoned 6 months.
Tried by the First London Jury before Mr. RECORDER.
